= -1/(1/3)
= -3

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the first line (through points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\)).**
2. **Determine the slope of the second line, knowing that it is perpendicular to the first.**
3. **Set up an equation using the slope formula for the second line (through points \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\)) and solve for \(x\).**

Let's go through each step in detail.

---

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope of the First Line**

The slope (\(m\)) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

For the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\):
\[
m_1 = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line**

If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes (\(m_1\) and \(m_2\)) satisfy:
\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]
Thus,
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1}
\]

Substituting \(m_1 = \frac{1}{3}\):
\[
m_2 = -\frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)} = -3
\]

**Step 3: Set Up the Equation for the Second Line**

The slope of the second line, passing through \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\), is:
\[
m_2 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

We know from Step 2 that \(m_2 = -3\). Therefore:
\[
\frac{12}{x - 10} = -3
\]

**Solve for \(x\):**
\[
12 = -3(x - 10)
\]
\[
12 = -3x + 30
\]
\[
-3x = 12 - 30
\]
\[
-3x = -18
\]
\[
x = \frac{-18}{-3} = 6
\]

---

**Final Answer**

The value of \(x\) is:
\[
\boxed{6}
\]

To find the area of the quadrilateral with vertices A(-6, 7), B(2, 9), C(7, -3), and D(-2, 0) using the shoelace formula, we follow these steps:

1. List the coordinates in order and repeat the first vertex at the end:
   \[
   (-6, 7), (2, 9), (7, -3), (-2, 0), (-6, 7)
   \]

2. Calculate the sum of the products of each x-coordinate and the y-coordinate of the next point:
   \[
   (-6) \cdot 9 + 2 \cdot (-3) + 7 \cdot 0 + (-2) \cdot 7 = -54 - 6 + 0 - 14 = -74
   \]

3. Calculate the sum of the products of each y-coordinate and the x-coordinate of the next point:
   \[
   7 \cdot 2 + 9 \cdot 7 + (-3) \cdot (-2) + 0 \cdot (-6) = 14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
   \]

4. Find the absolute difference between these two sums and divide by 2:
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} | -74 - 83 | = \frac{1}{2} | -157 | = \frac{157}{2} = 78.5
   \]

Thus, the area of the quadrilateral is \(\boxed{78.5}\).

The point on the x-axis equidistant from points A(9,8) and B(5,6) can be found by setting up an equation where the distances from this point to both A and B are equal. Solving this equation gives us the coordinates of the desired point.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Define the Point on X-axis:**
   Let the required point be P = (x, 0).

2. **Calculate Distances:**
   - Distance from P to A: \( \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} \)
   - Distance from P to B: \( \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36} \)

3. **Set Distances Equal and Square Both Sides:**
   \( (9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36 \)

4. **Expand and Simplify:**
   - Expand both sides:
     \( 81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36 \)
   - Combine like terms:
     \( 145 - 18x = 61 - 10x \)

5. **Solve for x:**
   Subtracting and simplifying gives \( x = \frac{21}{4} \) or 5.25.

6. **Final Coordinates of P:**
   \( P = (5.25, 0) \)

**Answer:**  
The point on the x-axis equidistant from A(9,8) and B(5,6) is at coordinates (21/4, 0).

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to figure out if three points form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem. The points are A(4,4), B(3,5), and C(-1,-1). 

I remember that in coordinate geometry, one way to determine if a triangle is right-angled is by checking if any two sides are perpendicular to each other. If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes will be negative reciprocals of each other. So, I think the plan is to calculate the slopes between these points and see if any pair satisfies that condition.

First, let me label the points: A(4,4), B(3,5), C(-1,-1). 

I need to find the slopes of AB, BC, and AC. The formula for slope between two points (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) is m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1).

Let me start with AB. So, A is (4,4) and B is (3,5). Plugging into the formula: m_AB = (5-4)/(3-4) = 1/(-1) = -1.

Next, BC connects points B(3,5) and C(-1,-1). Using the same slope formula: m_BC = (-1-5)/(-1-3) = (-6)/(-4) = 6/4. Simplifying that gives me 3/2.

Now, AC is between A(4,4) and C(-1,-1): m_AC = (-1 - 4)/(-1 - 4) = (-5)/(-5) = 1.

So now I have the slopes:
- AB has a slope of -1,
- BC has a slope of 3/2,
- AC has a slope of 1.

Now, to check for perpendicularity, I see if any pair of these slopes are negative reciprocals. The negative reciprocal of a number m is -1/m.

Let me check AB and AC first: m_AB = -1 and m_AC = 1. Multiplying them gives (-1)*(1) = -1. Since the product is -1, they are perpendicular! So that means angle at point A between points B and C is a right angle.

Just to be thorough, I can check if any other pair of slopes also satisfy this condition:
- AB slope -1 vs BC slope 3/2: (-1)*(3/2) = -3/2 ≠ -1.
- AC slope 1 vs BC slope 3/2: (1)*(3/2) = 3/2 ≠ -1.

So only the slopes of AB and AC are negative reciprocals, meaning they form a right angle at point A. Therefore, yes, these points do form a right-angled triangle.
</think>

To determine if the points \( A(4,4) \), \( B(3,5) \), and \( C(-1,-1) \) form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem, we can examine the slopes of the lines connecting these points. If two sides are perpendicular (i.e., their slopes multiply to -1), then the triangle is right-angled.

1. **Calculate Slopes:**
   - \( AB \): \( m_{AB} = \frac{5 - 4}{3 - 4} = -1 \)
   - \( BC \): \( m_{BC} = \frac{-1 - 5}{-1 - 3} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2} \)
   - \( AC \): \( m_{AC} = \frac{-1 - 4}{-1 - 4} = 1 \)

2. **Check for Perpendicularity:**
   - \( m_{AB} \times m_{AC} = (-1) \times (1) = -1 \), indicating perpendicular lines at point A.

Thus, the triangle is right-angled at point \( A \).

First, recall that the slope-intercept form is y = mx + b, where m represents the slope and b is the y-intercept.

To find the **equation of a line** with a given **slope (m)** and passing through a specific **point** \((x_1, y_1)\), we can use the **slope-intercept form** of a linear equation:

\[
y = mx + b
\]

where:
- \( m \) is the slope.
- \( b \) is the y-intercept.

### Given:
- **Slope (m):** \(-2\)
- **Point:** \((0, 5)\)

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Known Values:**
   - Slope (\( m \)) = \(-2\)
   - Point (\( x_1, y_1 \)) = (0, 5)

2. **Use the Slope-Intercept Form:**
   
   Since we have a point where \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 5 \), this directly gives us the y-intercept (\( b \)):
   
   \[
   y = mx + b \\
   5 = (-2)(0) + b \\
   5 = b
   \]
   
3. **Write the Equation of the Line:**
   
   Substitute \( m = -2 \) and \( b = 5 \) into the slope-intercept form:
   
   \[
   y = -2x + 5
   \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{y = -2x + 5}
\]

**Solution:**

To find the equation of the line passing through the points \((3, 1)\) and \((5, 7)\), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (\(m\)):**

   The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:

   \[
   m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation:**

   The point-slope form of a line is:
   
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using one of the points, say \((3, 1)\):

   \[
   y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**

   Simplify the equation:

   \[
   y - 1 = 3x - 9
   \]
   
   Add 1 to both sides:
   
   \[
   y = 3x - 8
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}

- **Y-intercept (b):** The line crosses the y-axis at \(y = -\frac{5}{4}\), so \(b = -\frac{5}{4}\).

The general equation of a line in slope-intercept form is:
\[ y = mx + b \]

### Final Answer

To determine the equation of the line for which \(\tan \theta = \frac{1}{6}\) and has an x-intercept at (6, 0), we start by noting that \(\tan \theta\) is the slope \(m\) of the line. Therefore, the slope \(m\) is \(\frac{1}{6}\).

Given the slope \(m = \frac{1}{6}\) and the point (6, 0), we use the point-slope form of the equation of a line:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting \(m = \frac{1}{6}\), \(x_1 = 6\), and \(y_1 = 0\):
\[
y - 0 = \frac{1}{6}(x - 6)
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is \(\boxed{y = \dfrac{1}{6}x - 1}\).

To determine how many litres of milk the store owner can sell weekly at ₹117 per litre, we'll follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Identify the Given Points**
We have two data points based on the price and quantity demanded:
- When \( P = ₹114 \) per litre, \( Q = 1080 \) litres.
- When \( P = ₹116 \) per litre, \( Q = 1320 \) litres.

### **Step 2: Calculate the Slope (b) of the Demand Curve**
The demand relationship is linear and can be represented as:
\[ Q = a - bP \]
Where:
- \( b \) is the slope.
- \( a \) is the intercept on the quantity axis when price (\( P \)) is zero.

First, calculate the change in quantity demanded (\( \Delta Q \)) and the change in price (\( \Delta P \)):
\[
\begin{align*}
\Delta Q &= 1320 - 1080 = 240 \text{ litres} \\
\Delta P &= ₹116 - ₹114 = ₹2
\end{align*}
\]
The slope \( b \) is given by:
\[
b = \frac{\Delta Q}{\Delta P} = \frac{240 \text{ litres}}{₹2} = 120 \text{ litres per rupee}
\]

### **Step 3: Determine the Intercept (a)**
Using one of the given points to find \( a \):
\[
Q = a - bP \\
1080 = a - 120(114) \\
1080 = a - 13680 \\
a = 1080 + 13680 \\
a = 14760
\]

### **Step 4: Formulate the Demand Equation**
Now, we have:
\[
Q = 14760 - 120P
\]

### **Step 5: Calculate Quantity Demanded at ₹117 per litre**
Plug \( P = ₹117 \) into the demand equation:
\[
Q = 14760 - 120(117) \\
Q = 14760 - 14040 \\
Q = 720
\]

### **Final Answer**
The store owner can sell **720** litres of milk weekly at ₹117 per litre.

The equation of the line can be found using the slope and a point on the line.

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular**: The given point where the perpendicular meets the line is (2, 13). Since this is the foot of the perpendicular from the origin, calculate its slope.
   
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = \frac{13 - 0}{2 - 0} = \frac{13}{2}
   \]

2. **Find the slope of the original line**: Since the two lines (the given perpendicular and our desired line) are perpendicular, their slopes multiply to -1.
   
   Let \( m_{\text{original}} \) be the slope we're looking for.

   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} \times m_{\text{original}} = -1
   \]

   Substitute the value:
   
   \[
   \frac{13}{2} \times m_{\text{original}} = -1 \\
   m_{\text{original}} = -\frac{2}{13}
   \]

3. **Use point-slope form to find the equation of the line**: The desired line passes through (2, 13) and has a slope of \( -\frac{2}{13} \).

   Apply the point-slope formula:

   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Substitute:
   
   \[
   y - 13 = -\frac{2}{13}(x - 2)
   \]

4. **Simplify to standard form**:
   
   Multiply both sides by 13 to eliminate the denominator:

   \[
   13(y - 13) = -2(x - 2) \\
   13y - 169 = -2x + 4
   \]
   
   Bring all terms to one side:
   
   \[
   2x + 13y - 173 = 0
   \]

To solve the problem, we determine the equation of a line passing through the point \((0, 2)\) and making an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians (or 120 degrees) with the positive x-axis. 

1. **Calculate the slope**:
   - The slope \( m \) is given by the tangent of the angle: 
     \[
     m = \tan\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)
     \]
   - Since \( \tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3} \), we have:
     \[
     m = -\sqrt{3}
     \]

2. **Find the equation of the first line**:
   - Using the point-slope form with the point \((0, 2)\):
     \[
     y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0)
     \]
   - Simplifying gives:
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2
     \]

3. **Determine the equation of the parallel line**:
   - Parallel lines have the same slope, so the second line also has a slope of \( m = -\sqrt{3} \).
   - This line crosses the y-axis at \( (0, -2) \), which means when \( x = 0 \), \( y = -2 \). Therefore, the y-intercept is \( b = -2 \).
   - The equation of the parallel line is:
     \[
     y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2
     \]

**Final Answer**:
- First Line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 2 \)
- Second Line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \)

Given the equation:

\[
4\sqrt{x} + \frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{1 - x}} = \sqrt{\frac{x}{x - 1}}
\]

We start by analyzing the left-hand side (LHS) and the right-hand side (RHS). The LHS consists of real terms, while the RHS involves a square root of a negative number when \(0 < x < 1\), making it an imaginary number. 

To elaborate, if \(x\) is such that \(0 < x < 1\), both \(\sqrt{x}\) and \(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{1 - x}}\) are real and positive, thus their sum (LHS) is a real number. However, the RHS becomes imaginary because the argument inside the square root becomes negative:

\[
\sqrt{\frac{x}{x - 1}} = \sqrt{-\left|\frac{x}{x - 1}\right|}
\]

This results in an imaginary number. Therefore, we are equating a real number (LHS) to an imaginary number (RHS), which is only possible if both sides are zero. However, the LHS cannot be zero because it is a sum of non-negative terms.

Additionally, considering \(x \geq 1\) results in undefined terms due to square roots of negative numbers or division by zero.

Thus, there are no real solutions for \(x\). The equation has no real solution.

\[
\boxed{\varnothing}
\]

To find the equation of the line passing through \((-5, 7)\) and perpendicular to the line through the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope (\(m\)) of the given line:**
   
   The formula for slope is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\):
   \[
   m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line:**
   
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.
   
   So, if \(m\) is \(-\frac{1}{9}\), then:
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\left(\frac{1}{-\frac{1}{9}}\right) = 9
   \]

3. **Use the point-slope form to find the equation of the perpendicular line:**
   
   The point-slope formula is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m_{\text{perpendicular}}(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the point \((-5, 7)\):
   \[
   y - 7 = 9(x - (-5)) \\
   y - 7 = 9(x + 5) \\
   y = 9x + 45 + 7 \\
   y = 9x + 52
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{y = 9x + 52}
\]

To find the equation of the median through vertex \( R(4, 5) \) in triangle \( PQR \) with vertices \( P(2, 1) \), \( Q(-2, 3) \), and \( R(4, 5) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint of Side \( PQ \)

The midpoint \( M \) of side \( PQ \) can be calculated using the midpoint formula:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the coordinates of points \( P(2, 1) \) and \( Q(-2, 3) \):
\[
M_x = \frac{2 + (-2)}{2} = \frac{0}{2} = 0
\]
\[
M_y = \frac{1 + 3}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
So, the midpoint \( M \) is at \( (0, 2) \).

### Step 2: Find the Equation of the Median \( RM \)

The median connects vertex \( R(4, 5) \) to the midpoint \( M(0, 2) \). To find the equation of this line:

1. **Calculate the Slope (\( m \))**:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{5 - 2}{4 - 0} = \frac{3}{4}
\]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form** to find the equation of the line, using point \( R(4, 5) \):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting the known values:
\[
y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}(x - 4)
\]

3. **Simplify** to get the equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)):
\[
y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}x - 3
\]
\[
y = \frac{3}{4}x + 2
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the median through vertex \( R \) is:
\[
\boxed{\, y = \dfrac{3}{4}x + 2 \,}
\]

Substituting the known values:
Distance = |5*5 + (-6)*(-7) + (-28)| / sqrt(5² + (-6)²)
= |25 + 42 - 28| / sqrt(25 + 36)
= |39| / sqrt(61)
= 39 / sqrt(61)

Distance = (39 * sqrt(61)) / 61

To find the distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry:

\[
d = \frac{|Ax_0 + By_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coefficients:**
   - From the equation \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we have:
     \[
     A = 5, \quad B = -6, \quad C = -28
     \]
   - The point is \((x_0, y_0) = (5, -7)\).

2. **Substitute the values into the formula:**
   \[
   d = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) - 28|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]

3. **Calculate the numerator:**
   \[
   |5 \times 5 + (-6) \times (-7) - 28| = |25 + 42 - 28| = |39|
   \]

4. **Calculate the denominator:**
   \[
   \sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61}
   \]

5. **Compute the distance:**
   \[
   d = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}}
   \]
   
6. **Rationalize the denominator (if needed):**
   \[
   d = \frac{39\sqrt{61}}{61}
   \]

7. **Approximate the value (optional):**
   \[
   \sqrt{61} \approx 7.81
   \]
   \[
   d \approx \frac{39 \times 7.81}{61} \approx \frac{304.59}{61} \approx 4.992 \text{ units}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\left\{ \text{Final Answer} : 4.992 \right\}}
\]

To find the distance between the parallel lines \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance between two parallel lines:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Here, \(A = 3\), \(B = -4\), \(C_1 = 12\), and \(C_2 = 7\). Plugging these into the formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|12 - 7|}{\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

Thus, the distance between the two lines is 1.

The foot of the perpendicular from point \( S(1, -2) \) to the line \( 3x - 4y = 16 \) is at coordinates \boxed{\left( \dfrac{8}{5}, -\dfrac{14}{5} \right)}.
</think>

Given the point \( S(1, -2) \) and the line \( 3x - 4y = 16 \), we need to find the foot of the perpendicular from \( S \) to this line.

1. **Find the slope of the given line**:
   The given line is \( 3x - 4y = 16 \). Converting it to slope-intercept form, we get \( y = \frac{3}{4}x - 4 \). Thus, the slope (\( m \)) of the line is \( \frac{3}{4} \).

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line**:
   The slope of the perpendicular line is the negative reciprocal of \( \frac{3}{4} \), which is \( -\frac{4}{3} \).

3. **Equation of the perpendicular line**:
   Using the point-slope form for the perpendicular line passing through \( S(1, -2) \):
   \[
   y - (-2) = -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1)
   \]
   Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   y + 2 = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3}
   \]
   Converting to slope-intercept form:
   \[
   y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3} - 2
   \]
   \[
   y = -\frac{4}{3}x - \frac{2}{3}
   \]

4. **Find the intersection of the two lines**:
   Set the equations of the original line and the perpendicular line equal to each other:
   \[
   \frac{3}{4}x - 4 = -\frac{4}{3}x - \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   Multiply through by 12 to clear the denominators:
   \[
   9x - 48 = -16x - 8
   \]
   Solving for \( x \):
   \[
   25x = 40 \implies x = \frac{40}{25} = \frac{8}{5}
   \]

5. **Find the corresponding \( y \)-coordinate**:
   Substitute \( x = \frac{8}{5} \) back into the equation of the original line:
   \[
   y = \frac{3}{4} \left( \frac{8}{5} \right) - 4
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   y = \frac{24}{20} - 4 = \frac{6}{5} - \frac{20}{5} = -\frac{14}{5}
   \]

Thus, the foot of the perpendicular from point \( S(1, -2) \) to the line \( 3x - 4y = 16 \) is at coordinates \(\boxed{\left( \dfrac{8}{5}, -\dfrac{14}{5} \right)}\).

Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find the equation of the right bisector of the line segment joining two points: (5, 6) and (1, 4). Hmm, let me break this down step by step.

First, what exactly is a right bisector? From my math class, I remember that a bisector is a line that divides another line into two equal parts. Specifically, the perpendicular bisector of a segment is a line that not only cuts the segment into two congruent halves but does so at a 90-degree angle. So, it's both a midpoint and perpendicular to the original segment.

Alright, so step one: find the midpoint of the given segment because the right bisector must pass through this midpoint. The midpoint formula is pretty straightforward; I think it's ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2). Let me apply that here.

Given points (5, 6) and (1, 4), so plugging into the midpoint formula: 

Midpoint M = [(5 + 1)/2, (6 + 4)/2] = [6/2, 10/2] = (3, 5). Okay, got that. The midpoint is at (3, 5).

Now, step two: find the slope of the original segment so I can determine the slope of the perpendicular bisector. The formula for slope m between two points (x₁, y₁) and (x₂, y₂) is (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁). Applying that here:

Slope m = (4 - 6)/(1 - 5) = (-2)/(-4) = 0.5 or 1/2.

So the slope of the original segment is 1/2. But since we need the perpendicular bisector, its slope will be the negative reciprocal of this. The negative reciprocal of 1/2 is -2 because flipping it gives 2 and changing the sign makes it -2.

Got that: the slope of the perpendicular bisector is -2.

Now, with the midpoint (3,5) and the slope -2, I can use the point-slope form to find the equation of the bisector. The point-slope formula is y - y₁ = m(x - x₁). Plugging in:

y - 5 = -2(x - 3)

Let me expand that: 

y - 5 = -2x + 6

Then, adding 5 to both sides gives:

y = -2x + 11

So the equation of the right bisector is y equals negative two x plus eleven.

Midpoint: (5+1)/2=3, (6+4)/2=5 → Correct.

To find the equation of the perpendicular bisector of the line segment joining (5, 6) and (1, 4):

1. **Find the midpoint**:  
   Midpoint formula: \(\left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)\).  
   Substituting values:  
   \[
   \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the slope of the segment**:  
   Slope formula: \(m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}\).  
   Substituting values:  
   \[
   m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

3. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular bisector**:  
   The negative reciprocal of \( \frac{1}{2} \) is \( -2 \).

4. **Use point-slope form to find the equation of the bisector**:  
   Point-slope formula: \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \).  
   Substituting midpoint (3, 5) and slope (-2):  
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
   \]
   
5. **Simplify to slope-intercept form**:  
   Expanding the equation:  
   \[
   y - 5 = -2x + 6 \\
   y = -2x + 11
   \]

The equation of the right bisector is \( y = -2x + 11 \).

The problem involves finding the value of \( h \) such that the line through points \( (h,5) \) and \( (6,3) \) intersects another given line at a right angle. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the slope of the given line \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \):**
   - Convert to slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \).
   - Subtract \( 7x \) and add 19: \( -9y = -7x + 19 \).
   - Divide by -9: \( y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9} \).
   - Slope (\( m_1 \)) is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line (\( m_2 \)):**
   - Perpendicular slopes are negative reciprocals.
   - Reciprocal of \( \frac{7}{9} \) is \( \frac{9}{7} \).
   - Negative reciprocal: \( m_2 = -\frac{9}{7} \).

3. **Calculate the slope between points \( (h,5) \) and \( (6,3) \):**
   - Use the formula for slope: \( \text{slope} = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \).
   - Substitute values: \( m = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - h} = \frac{-2}{6 - h} \).

4. **Set the calculated slope equal to \( -\frac{9}{7} \):**
   - Equation: \( \frac{-2}{6 - h} = -\frac{9}{7} \).
   
5. **Solve for \( h \):**
   - Cross-multiplied: \( (-2)(7) = (-9)(6 - h) \).
   - Simplify: \( -14 = -54 + 9h \).
   - Add 54 to both sides: \( 40 = 9h \).
   - Divide by 9: \( h = \frac{40}{9} \).

**Final Answer:**  
The value of \( h \) is \( \boxed{\dfrac{40}{9}} \).

**Solution:**

We are tasked with finding the equation of a line that is perpendicular to the line given by \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) and has an x-intercept at \( (5, 0) \).

1. **Find the Slope of the Given Line (\( m_1 \)):**

   Start with the equation:
   \[
   x - 7y + 5 = 0
   \]
   
   Rearrange to solve for \( y \):
   \[
   -7y = -x - 5 \\
   y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7}
   \]
   
   Thus, the slope (\( m_1 \)) of the given line is:
   \[
   m_1 = \frac{1}{7}
   \]

2. **Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line (\( m_2 \)):**

   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.
   \[
   m_2 = -\frac{1}{m_1} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{7}} = -7
   \]

3. **Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line:**

   We know the x-intercept is \( (5, 0) \). Using this point and the slope \( m_2 = -7 \), apply the point-slope formula:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Substituting \( x_1 = 5 \) and \( y_1 = 0 \):
   \[
   y - 0 = -7(x - 5) \\
   y = -7x + 35
   \]

4. **Express the Equation in Standard Form:**

   Rearrange to get all terms on one side:
   \[
   7x + y = 35
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{7x + y = 35}

To find the equation of a line that is parallel to the given line \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passes through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Slope of the Given Line

First, rewrite the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)) to find its slope.

\[
5x - 6y + 4 = 0
\]
\[
-6y = -5x - 4 \quad \Rightarrow \quad y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
\]

The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

### Step 2: Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Parallel Line

Since parallel lines have the same slope, the desired line will also have a slope of \(\frac{5}{6}\). Using the point-slope form:

\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]

Substitute \(m = \frac{5}{6}\), and the coordinates of the given point \((-4, 5)\):

\[
y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
\]

### Step 3: Simplify to Standard Form

Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate the fraction:

\[
6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4)
\]
\[
6y - 30 = 5x + 20
\]

Rearrange terms to get the equation in standard form (\(Ax + By + C = 0\)):

\[
5x - 6y + 50 = 0 \quad \text{(This step seems incorrect based on the calculation above)}
\]
However, upon reviewing our steps, we realize a mistake was made during simplification. Let's correct that.

Starting from:
\[
y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
\]

Multiply both sides by 6:

\[
6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4)
\]
\[
6y - 30 = 5x + 20
\]

Bring all terms to one side:

\[
-5x + 6y - 50 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 5x - 6y + 50 = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the desired line is:
\[
\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}
\]

To find the distance between the lines \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\) and \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\), we first normalize the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) for both equations.

The first line is already given as:
\[ 38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \]

The second line can be rewritten by dividing all terms by 2:
\[ 19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \]

Now, both equations have the same coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\):
- First line: \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\)
- Second line: \(19x + 12y + 35 = 0\)

Using the formula for the distance between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\), which is:
\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Here, \(C_1 = -38\) and \(C_2 = 35\). Plugging these values into the formula, we get:
\[ |C_1 - C_2| = |-38 - 35| = 73 \]
\[ A^2 + B^2 = 19^2 + 12^2 = 361 + 144 = 505 \]

Thus, the distance is:
\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \]

Rationalizing the denominator, we get:
\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505} \]

The exact distance between the two lines is:
\[ \boxed{\dfrac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}} \]

First, I need to understand what "intercept form" means for a linear equation. Intercept form typically expresses a line in terms of its x-intercept and y-intercept.

The given equation is 6x + 4y – 24 = 0. To convert this into intercept form, I'll rearrange it so that both variables are on one side and the constant term is isolated on the other.

I'll move the constant to the right side: 6x + 4y = 24.

Next, I'll divide every term by 24 to normalize the equation:
(6x)/24 + (4y)/24 = 24/24
This simplifies to x/4 + y/6 = 1.

Now that it's in intercept form, I can identify the intercepts. The x-intercept is the value of x when y=0, which corresponds to the term with 'a' or 'x'. Here, a = 4.

Similarly, the y-intercept is the value of y when x=0, corresponding to the term with 'b' or 'y'. Here, b = 6.

Thus, the intercept form of the equation is x/4 + y/6 = 1, with an x-intercept at (4, 0) and a y-intercept at (0, 6).
</think>

To reduce the equation \(6x + 4y - 24 = 0\) into intercept form and find their intercepts on the axes, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rearrange the Equation
Start by moving all terms except the constant to one side:
\[
6x + 4y = 24
\]

### Step 2: Divide Both Sides by 24
This step converts the equation into intercept form \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1\), where \(a\) is the x-intercept and \(b\) is the y-intercept.

Divide every term by 24:
\[
\frac{6x}{24} + \frac{4y}{24} = \frac{24}{24}
\]

Simplify the fractions:
\[
\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1
\]

### Step 3: Identify the Intercepts
From the equation \(\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1\):

- **X-intercept (\(a\))**: Set \(y = 0\) and solve for \(x\):
  \[
  \frac{x}{4} = 1 \Rightarrow x = 4
  \]
  
- **Y-intercept (\(b\))**: Set \(x = 0\) and solve for \(y\):
  \[
  \frac{y}{6} = 1 \Rightarrow y = 6
  \]

To express the equation \( 12x - 9y = 18 \) in intercept form:

1. **Find the x-intercept** by setting \( y = 0 \):
   \[
   12x = 18 \implies x = \frac{18}{12} = \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   
2. **Find the y-intercept** by setting \( x = 0 \):
   \[
   -9y = 18 \implies y = -2
   \]

3. Write the intercept form using these intercepts:
   \[
   \frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
   \]
   
This can be simplified to:
\[
\frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
The equation in intercept form is \boxed{\dfrac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \dfrac{y}{-2} = 1}.

\[ 9x + 6y - 7 = 0 \]
and
\[ 3x + 2y + 6 = 0 \]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Ensure Both Lines Have the Same Coefficients for \(x\) and \(y\):**
   
   The second equation, \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\), can be scaled to match the coefficients of the first equation by multiplying it by 3:
   
   \[ 9x + 6y + 18 = 0 \]

2. **Identify the General Form for a Parallel Line:**
   
   Since both lines are parallel, they have the same slope. We can represent any line equidistant from these two as:
   
   \[ 9x + 6y + C = 0 \]
   
   Our goal is to find the value of \(C\).

3. **Calculate the Distance Between Two Parallel Lines:**
   
   The formula for the distance between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\) is:
   
   \[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|C_2 - C_1|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]
   
4. **Set Up Equations for Equidistance:**
   
   To find the line equidistant from both original lines, it must be exactly halfway between them. This means:
   
   \[ \frac{| -7 + C |}{\sqrt{9^2 + 6^2}} = \frac{|18 - (-C)|}{\sqrt{9^2 + 6^2}} \]
   
   Simplifying both sides, we get:
   
   \[ |-7 + C| = |18 + C| \]
   
5. **Solve for \(C\):**
   
   Considering the absolute value equations:
   
   - Case 1: \(-7 + C = 18 + C\) leads to a contradiction.
   - Case 2: \(-7 + C = -(18 + C)\)
     
     Solving this gives:
     
     \[ -7 + C = -18 - C \]
     \[ 2C = -11 \]
     \[ C = -\frac{11}{2} \]

6. **Substitute \(C\) Back into the General Form:**
   
   Plugging \(C = -\frac{5}{2}\) (simplifying from step above):
   
   \[ 9x + 6y - \frac{5}{2} = 0 \]
   
7. **Final Equation:**

   The equation of the line equidistant from both given parallel lines is:
   
   \[
   \boxed{9x + 6y - \dfrac{5}{2} = 0}

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

**Step 1: Find the slope of the first line**

We have two points on the first line:
- Point A: \((0, 8)\)
- Point B: \((6, 10)\)

The formula for the slope (\(m\)) between two points is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the values:
\[
m_{\text{first line}} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the slope of the perpendicular line**

If two lines are perpendicular, their slopes (\(m_1\) and \(m_2\)) satisfy the relationship:
\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]
So,
\[
\frac{1}{3} \times m_2 = -1 \\
m_2 = -3
\]

**Step 3: Use the second line's slope to find \(x\)**

We have two points on the second line:
- Point C: \((10, 14)\)
- Point D: \((x, 26)\)

Using the slope formula again for these two points and knowing that the slope is \(-3\):
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \\
-3 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} \\
-3 = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \(x\)**
\[
-3(x - 10) = 12 \\
-3x + 30 = 12 \\
-3x = 12 - 30 \\
-3x = -18 \\
x = \frac{-18}{-3} \\
x = 6

Let me calculate the distance from (5a, 0) to (9, 8):

Distance1 = sqrt[(9 - 5a)^2 + (8 - 0)^2]
= sqrt[(9 - 5a)^2 + 64]

Similarly, the distance from (5a, 0) to (5, 6):

Distance2 = sqrt[(5 - 5a)^2 + (6 - 0)^2]
= sqrt[(5 - 5a)^2 + 36]

Since these distances are equal, I'll set them equal to each other:

sqrt[(9 - 5a)^2 + 64] = sqrt[(5 - 5a)^2 + 36]

To eliminate the square roots, I'll square both sides of the equation:

(9 - 5a)^2 + 64 = (5 - 5a)^2 + 36

Now, expand both squares:

For (9 - 5a)^2: 81 - 90a + 25a^2
For (5 - 5a)^2: 25 - 50a + 25a^2

Plugging these back into the equation:

(81 - 90a + 25a^2) + 64 = (25 - 50a + 25a^2) + 36

Simplify both sides:

Left side: 81 + 64 = 145, so 25a^2 - 90a + 145
Right side: 25 + 36 = 61, so 25a^2 - 50a + 61

Now the equation is:

25a^2 - 90a + 145 = 25a^2 - 50a + 61

Subtract 25a^2 from both sides to eliminate that term:

-90a + 145 = -50a + 61

Now, let's get all terms involving 'a' on one side and constants on the other. I'll add 90a to both sides and subtract 61 from both sides:

145 - 61 = 90a - 50a
84 = 40a

a = 84 / 40

a = 21/10 or 2.1

So, a is equal to 21/10.

To find the slope of the line passing through the origin (0, 0) and the midpoint of segment PB, where P(2, -2) and B(10, 2):

1. **Find the Midpoint**:  
   The midpoint M is calculated as:  
   \[
   M = \left(\frac{2 + 10}{2}, \frac{-2 + 2}{2}\right) = (6, 0)
   \]

2. **Calculate Slope Between Origin and Midpoint**:  
   Using the slope formula between two points (x₁, y₁) and (x₂, y₂):  
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - 0}{6 - 0} = 0
   \]

First, I need to determine if the given points form a right-angled triangle without using the Pythagorean theorem. The points provided are (4, 4), (3, 5), and (-1, -1).

To do this, I'll calculate the slopes of each pair of points because in a right-angled triangle, two sides must be perpendicular, meaning their slopes will multiply to -1.

Calculating Slopes:
1. Slope between (4, 4) and (3, 5):
Slope = (5 - 4)/(3 - 4) = 1/(-1) = -1

2. Slope between (3, 5) and (-1, -1):
Slope = (-1 - 5)/(-1 - 3) = (-6)/(-4) = 3/2

3. Slope between (-1, -1) and (4, 4):
Slope = (4 - (-1))/(4 - (-1)) = 5/5 = 1

Checking for Perpendicularity:
- Multiply the first slope by the third slope: (-1) * 1 = -1
Since this product equals -1, the sides with slopes -1 and 1 are perpendicular.

Therefore, the triangle formed by these points is a right-angled triangle.

To find the angle between the x-axis and the line joining two points (5, 1) and (6, 0), I'll start by determining the slope of the line.

First, calculate the change in y (Δy) and Δx:
Δy = 0 - 1 = -1
Δx = 6 - 5 = 1

Next, compute the slope (m):
m = Δy / Δx = -1/1 = -1

Now that I have the slope, I can find the angle using the arctangent function:
θ = arctan(m) = arctan(-1)

The arctangent of -1 is -45 degrees. However, since angles are typically reported as positive values between 0 and 90 degrees when measuring from the x-axis to a line in standard position, I'll convert the negative angle by adding 360 degrees:
-45 + 360 = 315 degrees

Thus, the angle between the x-axis and the given line is 315 degrees.
</think>

To find the **angle between the x-axis** and the **line joining the points (5, 1) and (6, 0)**, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope of the Line
The slope (\( m \)) of a line passing through two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Given Points:**
- Point A: \( (5, 1) \)
- Point B: \( (6, 0) \)

Plugging in the values:
\[
m = \frac{0 - 1}{6 - 5} = \frac{-1}{1} = -1
\]

### Step 2: Find the Angle Using Arctangent
The angle (\( \theta \)) between the line and the positive x-axis can be found using the arctangent of the slope:
\[
\theta = \arctan(m)
\]
\[
\theta = \arctan(-1) = -45^\circ
\]

Since angles are typically measured as positive values from the x-axis, we'll consider the angle in the standard position. The negative angle indicates direction, so adding 360 degrees gives:
\[
\theta = -45^\circ + 360^\circ = 315^\circ
\]

Given that one line has a slope double the other and the angle between them satisfies \(\tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3}\), we need to find the slopes of these lines.

First, let \(m_1\) be the slope of one line and \(m_2 = 2m_1\) be the slope of the other line. The tangent of the angle between two lines with slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\) is given by:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
Substituting \(m_2 = 2m_1\), we get:
\[
\left| \frac{2m_1 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot 2m_1} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Simplifying this, we have:
\[
\left| \frac{m_1}{1 + 2m_1^2} \right| = \frac{1}{3}
\]
This leads to two equations by considering the absolute value:
\[
\frac{m_1}{1 + 2m_1^2} = \frac{1}{3} \quad \text{or} \quad \frac{m_1}{1 + 2m_1^2} = -\frac{1}{3}
\]

Solving these equations, we find:
1. For the positive case:
   \[
   m_1 = \frac{1}{2} (1 + 2m_1^2)
   \]
   Solving this quadratic equation, we get \(m_1 = \frac{1}{2}\) or \(m_1 = 1\).

2. For the negative case:
   \[
   m_1 = -\frac{1}{3} (1 + 2m_1^2)
   \]
   Solving this quadratic equation, we get \(m_1 = -\frac{1}{2}\) or \(m_1 = -1\).

Thus, the possible slopes for one line are \(\frac{1}{2}, 1, -\frac{1}{2},\) and \(-1\). Correspondingly, the slopes of the other line (which is double) will be \(1, 2, -1,\) and \(-2\).

Therefore, the pairs of slopes are:
- \(\left( \frac{1}{2}, 1 \right)\)
- \(\left( -\frac{1}{2}, -1 \right)\)
- \((1, 2)\)
- \((-1, -2)\)

The possible pairs of slopes are:
\[
\boxed{\left( \frac{1}{2}, 1 \right)}, \boxed{\left( -\frac{1}{2}, -1 \right)}, \boxed{(1, 2)}, \text{ and } \boxed{(-1, -2)}
\]

First off, I remember that there are two main coordinate axes: the x-axis and the y-axis. Lines parallel to these would either be horizontal or vertical. 

Starting with the line that's parallel to the x-axis. If a line is parallel to the x-axis, it means it has the same y-coordinate for all its points because it doesn't rise or fall—it's flat. So, any point on this line will have a constant y-value.

Since the line passes through (0, 5), that gives me the specific y-value. In this case, every point on the line must have y = 5 regardless of what x is. Therefore, the equation of this line should be straightforward: y equals some constant. Given that it goes through (0,5), the constant has to be 5. So, the equation would be y = 5.

Now, moving on to the line that's parallel to the y-axis. I recall that lines parallel to the y-axis are vertical lines. These lines have a fixed x-value because they don't move left or right—only up and down.

   Let's first find the equation of the line passing through the points \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\).

   \[

   \]

Alright, so I've got this problem where a milk store owner sells different quantities of milk based on the price per litre. At ₹114 per litre, he sells 1080 litres weekly, and at ₹116 per litre, he sells 1320 litres weekly. Now, they're asking how many litres he can sell if the price is set at ₹117 per litre.

Okay, first off, this seems like a problem involving linear relationships between two variables: price (x-axis) and quantity demanded (y-axis). Since it's stated to assume a linear relationship, I think we'll model this with a straight line equation.

So, let me recall that the general form of a linear equation is:

\[ y = mx + b \]

Where:
- \( m \) is the slope,
- \( b \) is the y-intercept.

In this context, \( x \) represents the price per litre, and \( y \) represents the quantity demanded in litres.

To find the equation of the line, I'll need to determine two things: the slope (\( m \)) and the y-intercept (\( b \)). Once we have that, we can plug in the price (₹117) to find out how many litres will be sold at that price.

Alright, so first step is to calculate the slope. The formula for slope when given two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is:

\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]

Given:
- When the price was ₹114 (\( x_1 = 114 \)), quantity sold was 1080 litres (\( y_1 = 1080 \)).
- When the price increased to ₹116 (\( x_2 = 116 \)), the quantity sold increased to 1320 litres (\( y_2 = 1320 \)).

Plugging these into the slope formula:

\[ m = \frac{1320 - 1080}{116 - 114} = \frac{240}{2} = 120 \]

So, the slope \( m \) is 120. This means that for every increase of ₹1 in price, the quantity demanded increases by 120 litres.

Now that we have the slope, let's find the y-intercept (\( b \)). We can use one of the given points to solve for \( b \). Let's use \( (x_1, y_1) = (114, 1080) \).

Substituting into the equation:

\[ 1080 = 120 \times 114 + b \]

Calculating \( 120 \times 114 \):

\[ 120 \times 100 = 12,000 \]
\[ 120 \times 14 = 1,680 \]
\[ Total = 12,000 + 1,680 = 13,680 \]

So,

\[ 1080 = 13,680 + b \]

Now, solving for \( b \):

\[ b = 1080 - 13,680 = -12,600 \]

Therefore, the y-intercept is -12,600.

Wait a minute—that negative intercept seems odd in this context. Usually, at a price of ₹0 per litre (which doesn't make practical sense), we'd have some positive quantity demanded. However, mathematically, it's just part of the line equation based on our two given points.

So, with both \( m \) and \( b \) determined, our linear equation is:

\[ y = 120x - 12,600 \]

Now, to find out how many litres will be sold at ₹117 per litre (\( x = 117 \)):

Substitute \( x = 117 \) into the equation:

\[ y = 120 \times 117 - 12,600 \]

Calculating \( 120 \times 117 \):

\[ 120 \times 100 = 12,000 \]
\[ 120 \times 17 = 2,040 \]
\[ Total = 12,000 + 2,040 = 14,040 \]

Now,

\[ y = 14,040 - 12,600 = 1,440 \]

So, according to the linear model, at a price of ₹117 per litre, the milk owner can expect to sell **1,440 litres** weekly.

But wait—this is an extrapolation beyond our given data points. The two original prices were at ₹114 and ₹116, with quantities sold being 1,080 and 1,320 respectively. So, trying to predict the quantity demanded at a slightly different price (₹117) introduces some uncertainty because it's outside the range of our data.

To get a more accurate prediction within the given data range, we could use **Linear Interpolation** between the two known points instead. Let's try that for better precision.

### Using Linear Interpolation

The formula for linear interpolation is:

\[ y = y_1 + \left( \frac{(x - x_1)(y_2 - y_1)}{x_2 - x_1} \right) \]

Given:
- \( x_1 = 114 \), \( y_1 = 1080 \)
- \( x_2 = 116 \), \( y_2 = 1320 \)
- \( x = 117 \)

First, calculate the difference in price:

\[ x - x_1 = 117 - 114 = 3 \]

Then, calculate the slope (\( m \)) as before:

\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{1320 - 1080}{116 - 114} = 120 \]

Now, plug into the interpolation formula:

\[ y = 1080 + (3)(120) \]
\[ y = 1080 + 360 \]
\[ y = 1440 \]

So, using linear interpolation, we also get **1,440 litres** sold at ₹117 per litre. This aligns with our previous calculation from the general linear equation.

But it's important to note that this prediction is based solely on the assumption of a linear relationship between price and quantity demanded. Real-world demand can be influenced by various factors like consumer preferences, income levels, availability of substitutes, etc., making the demand relationship potentially non-linear or subject to change under different conditions.

In conclusion, using both the general linear equation approach and linear interpolation, we estimate that at a price of ₹117 per litre, the milk owner can expect to sell approximately **1,440 litres** weekly.
</think>

Now, let's break it down step by step:

### Step 1: Understand the Relationship
We have two points:
- When \( x = 114 \), \( y = 1080 \)
- When \( x = 116 \), \( y = 1320 \)

This indicates a linear relationship between price (\( x \)) and quantity demanded (\( y \)).

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope
The slope (\( m \)) of the line is calculated as:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{1320 - 1080}{116 - 114} = \frac{240}{2} = 120 \]

### Step 3: Determine the Y-Intercept
Using one of the points, say \( (114, 1080) \):
\[ y = m x + b \]
\[ 1080 = 120(114) + b \]
\[ 1080 = 13680 + b \]
\[ b = 1080 - 13680 \]
\[ b = -12600 \]

### Step 4: Formulate the Linear Equation
Now, the equation is:
\[ y = 120x - 12600 \]

### Step 5: Predict Quantity Demanded at \( x = 117 \)
Substitute \( x = 117 \) into the equation:
\[ y = 120(117) - 12600 \]
\[ y = 14040 - 12600 \]
\[ y = 1440 \]

### Final Answer

**1,440 litres**

Firstly, I understand that we are dealing with a straight line that makes equal intercepts on both the x-axis and y-axis. This means if the x-intercept is \(a\), then the y-intercept is also \(a\).

The general equation of a line in intercept form is \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1\). Since the intercepts are equal (\(a = b\)), this simplifies to \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{a} = 1\) or \(x + y = a\).

Given that the line passes through the point (4,5), substituting these values into the equation gives:
\[4 + 5 = a\]
Thus, \(a = 9\).

Finally, substituting \(a\) back into the simplified equation results in the final equation of the line being:
\[x + y = 9\]

Therefore, the solution is \(\boxed{x+y=9}\).
</think>

To find the equation of the straight line that makes equal intercepts on both axes and passes through the point (4,5), follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Intercepts:**
   - Let the x-intercept be \( a \). Since the intercepts are equal, the y-intercept is also \( a \).

2. **Equation of the Line in Intercept Form:**
   The general equation for a line with intercepts \( a \) on both axes is:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{a} = 1
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the Equation:**
   Combine the terms since the denominators are equal:
   \[
   \frac{x + y}{a} = 1 \implies x + y = a
   \]

4. **Substitute the Given Point (4,5):**
   Since the line passes through (4,5), substitute \( x = 4 \) and \( y = 5 \) into the equation:
   \[
   4 + 5 = a
   \]
   
5. **Solve for \( a \):**
   \[
   a = 9
   \]

6. **Final Equation of the Line:**
   Substitute \( a = 9 \) back into the simplified equation:
   \[
   x + y = 9
   \]

Therefore, the equation of the line is:

\[
\boxed{x+y=9}
\]

To find the equation of the line passing through the point \((-5, 7)\) and perpendicular to the line passing through the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the given line**:
   
   The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated using:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\):
   \[
   m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
   \]

2. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line**:
   
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\left(-\frac{9}{1}\right) = 9
   \]

3. **Use the point-slope form to find the equation**:
   
   The point-slope form of a line is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the slope \(m = 9\) and the point \((-5, 7)\):
   \[
   y - 7 = 9(x + 5)
   \]
   
4. **Simplify to get the final equation**:
   
   Expanding the equation:
   \[
   y - 7 = 9x + 45
   \]
   \[
   y = 9x + 52
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{y = 9x + 52}

The distance between the two parallel lines can be calculated using the formula for the distance between two parallel lines. Here's the step-by-step solution:

**Given Equations:**
- Line 1: \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\)
- Line 2: \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\)

**Formula for Distance Between Parallel Lines:**
The distance (\(d\)) between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\) is given by:
\[ d = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

**Applying the Values:**
- \(A = 3\), \(B = -4\)
- \(C_1 = 12\), \(C_2 = 7\)

Calculate numerator:
\[ |12 - 7| = 5 \]

Calculate denominator:
\[ \sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5 \]

**Distance:**
\[ d = \frac{5}{5} = 1 \]

The problem involves finding the values of \( m \) and \( c \) for the line \( y = mx + c \), given that the perpendicular from the origin to this line intersects it at the point \( (2, 4) \).

1. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular line:**
   - The slope of the original line is \( m \).
   - The slope of the perpendicular line is \( -\frac{1}{m} \).

2. **Equation of the perpendicular line passing through the origin:**
   - The equation is \( y = -\frac{1}{m}x \).

3. **Substitute the intersection point (2, 4) into both equations:**
   - For the original line:
     \[
     4 = 2m + c
     \]
   - For the perpendicular line:
     \[
     4 = -\frac{1}{m} \cdot 2
     \]

4. **Solve for \( m \):**
   From the perpendicular line equation:
   \[
   4 = -\frac{2}{m} \\
   m = -\frac{2}{4} \\
   m = -\frac{1}{2}
   \]

5. **Substitute \( m = -\frac{1}{2} \) into the original line equation to find \( c \):**
   \[
   4 = 2 \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + c \\
   4 = -1 + c \\
   c = 5
   \]

To find the equation of the right (perpendicular) bisector of the line segment joining the points \((5, 6)\) and \((1, 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint
The midpoint \(M\) of a line segment with endpoints \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is calculated as:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Original Line Segment
The slope \(m\) between two points is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
For our points:
\[
m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = 0.5
\]

### Step 3: Find the Slope of the Perpendicular Bisector
The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal of its slope:
\[
m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{m}
\]
So,
\[
m_{\text{perp}} = -\frac{1}{0.5} = -2
\]

### Step 4: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Bisector
The point-slope form of a line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using the midpoint \( (3, 5) \) and the perpendicular slope \( -2 \):
\[
y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
\]

### Step 5: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 5 = -2x + 6 \\
y = -2x + 11
\]

To solve for \( h \), we recognize that the line through points \( (h, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \) must be perpendicular to the given line \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \). 

First, calculate the slope of the given line. Rewriting it in slope-intercept form:
\[
7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \\
-9y = -7x + 19 \\
y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9}
\]
The slope \( m_2 \) is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

Next, find the slope of the line through \( (h, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \):
\[
m_1 = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - h} = \frac{-2}{6 - h}
\]

Since the lines are perpendicular:
\[
m_1 \cdot m_2 = -1 \\
\left( \frac{-2}{6 - h} \right) \cdot \left( \frac{7}{9} \right) = -1
\]
Simplify and solve for \( h \):
\[
\frac{-14}{9(6 - h)} = -1 \\
-14 = -9(6 - h) \\
-14 = -54 + 9h \\
40 = 9h \\
h = \frac{40}{9}
\]

The value of \( h \) is \( \frac{40}{9} \).

To determine the angle between the two lines given by \( y = -\sqrt{3}x + 1 \) and \( y = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}x + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \), we use the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \):

\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

First, identify the slopes:
- The slope of the first line is \( m_1 = -\sqrt{3} \).
- The slope of the second line is \( m_2 = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \).

Substitute these values into the formula:

\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + (-\sqrt{3})(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}})} \right|
\]

Simplify the numerator and denominator step by step:

1. Numerator:
   \[
   -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} - (-\sqrt{3}) = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} + \sqrt{3} = \frac{\sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}
   \]
2. Combine terms in the numerator:
   \[
   \sqrt{3} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{3 - 1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
   \]

3. Denominator:
   \[
   1 + (-\sqrt{3})(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}) = 1 + 1 = 2
   \]

Thus, we have:

\[
\tan \theta = \left| \frac{\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}}{2} \right| = \left| \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \right| = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
\]

Since \( \tan 30^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{3} \), we find that:

\[
\theta = 30^\circ
\]

The angle between the two lines is \(\boxed{30^\circ}\).

To find the equation of a line perpendicular to \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) with an x-intercept at 5, follow these steps:

1. **Determine the slope of the given line**:
   - Rewrite \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)):
     \[
     x - 7y + 5 = 0 \\
     -7y = -x - 5 \\
     y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7}
     \]
   - The slope (\( m_1 \)) of the given line is \( \frac{1}{7} \).

2. **Find the slope of the perpendicular line**:
   - The slope of a perpendicular line (\( m_2 \)) satisfies \( m_1 \times m_2 = -1 \):
     \[
     \left(\frac{1}{7}\right) \times m_2 = -1 \\
     m_2 = -7
     \]

3. **Use the point-slope form to find the equation**:
   - The x-intercept is (5, 0). Using \( m = -7 \):
     \[
     y - 0 = -7(x - 5) \\
     y = -7x + 35
     \]

4. **Express in standard form**:
   - Rearranging gives:
     \[
     7x + y = 35
     \]

**Final Answer**: The equation of the perpendicular line is \( 7x + y = 35 \).

To find the equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the original line**:
   - Start by rewriting the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
     \[
     5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \\
     -6y = -5x - 4 \\
     y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
     \]
   - **Slope (\(m\))**: \(\frac{5}{6}\)

2. **Use the slope for the parallel line**:
   - Parallel lines have identical slopes, so the slope of the new line is also \(\frac{5}{6}\).

3. **Apply the point-slope form**:
   - Use the point-slope formula to write the equation of the line passing through \((-4, 5)\):
     \[
     y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
     \]
     where \(m = \frac{5}{6}\), \(x_1 = -4\), and \(y_1 = 5\).

   - **Substitute the values**:
     \[
     y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x - (-4)) \\
     y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
     \]

4. **Simplify to slope-intercept form (if needed)**:
   - Distribute and simplify:
     \[
     y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6} \\
     y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3}
     \]
   
   - Add \(5\) to both sides:
     \[
     y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3} + 5
     \]
   
   - Convert \(5\) to a fraction with denominator \(3\):
     \[
     y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3} + \frac{15}{3}
     \]
   
   - Combine the constants:
     \[
     y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{y = \dfrac{5}{6}x + \dfrac{25}{3}}

First, I need to reduce the given equation \(6x + 4y - 24 = 0\) into intercept form. The intercept form of a linear equation is \(\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1\), where \(a\) and \(b\) are the x-intercept and y-intercept, respectively.

To do this, I'll divide each term in the equation by the constant term on the right side. So, dividing both sides by -24 gives:

\[
\frac{6x}{-24} + \frac{4y}{-24} = 1
\]

Simplifying the fractions:

\[
\frac{x}{-4} + \frac{y}{-6} = 1
\]

This can be rewritten as:

\[
\frac{x}{-4} + \frac{y}{-6} = 1
\]

</think>

To solve the problem of reducing the given equation into intercept form and finding their intercepts on the axes:

**Given Equation:**
\[ 6x + 4y - 24 = 0 \]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Convert to Intercept Form:**

   The general intercept form of a linear equation is:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
   \]
   where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.

2. **Rearrange the Equation:**
   
   Start by moving the constant term to the other side:
   \[
   6x + 4y = 24
   \]

3. **Divide Both Sides by 24:**

   To get the equation into intercept form, divide each term by the constant on the right side (24):
   \[
   \frac{6x}{24} + \frac{4y}{24} = \frac{24}{24}
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   \[
   \frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1
   \]

4. **Identify the Intercepts:**

   - **X-Intercept (\( a \)):** Set \( y = 0 \):
     \[
     \frac{x}{4} = 1 \implies x = 4
     \]
     
   - **Y-Intercept (\( b \)):** Set \( x = 0 \):
     \[
     \frac{y}{6} = 1 \implies y = 6
     \]

### Final Answer:

The equation in intercept form is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1}
\]

**Intercepts:**
- **X-Intercept:** \( (4, 0) \)
- **Y-Intercept:** \( (0, 6) \)

To solve the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\) and convert it into intercept form, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Intercept Form

The intercept form of a linear equation is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
where \(a\) is the x-intercept and \(b\) is the y-intercept.

### Step 2: Convert the Given Equation to Intercept Form

Starting with:
\[
12x - 9y = 18
\]

Divide every term by 18 to simplify:
\[
\frac{12x}{18} - \frac{9y}{18} = \frac{18}{18}
\]
Simplify the fractions:
\[
\frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
\]

Rewrite it to match the intercept form:
\[
\frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
\]

### Step 3: Identify the Intercepts

From the equation above, we can see that:

- **X-intercept (\(a\))**: The denominator under \(x\) is \(\frac{3}{2}\). Therefore:
  \[
  a = \frac{3}{2} = 1.5
  \]

- **Y-intercept (\(b\))**: The denominator under \(y\) is \(-2\). Therefore:
  \[
  b = -2
  \]

### Final Answer

The intercepts of the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\) are:

- X-intercept: \(1.5\)
- Y-intercept: \(-2\)

The distance from point \( A(1,2) \) to line \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \) along the line \( y = 2x \) can be found as follows:

1. **Find Intersection Point:**
   Substitute \( y = 2x \) into \( 4x + 7y + 5 = 0 \):
   \[
   4x + 7(2x) + 5 = 0 \\
   4x + 14x + 5 = 0 \\
   18x = -5 \\
   x = -\frac{5}{18} \\
   y = 2\left(-\frac{5}{18}\right) = -\frac{5}{9}
   \]
   Intersection point: \( \left(-\frac{5}{18}, -\frac{5}{9}\right) \).

2. **Calculate Distance Between Points \( (1,2) \) and \( \left(-\frac{5}{18}, -\frac{5}{9}\right) \):**
   Using the distance formula:
   \[
   d = \sqrt{\left(1 + \frac{5}{18}\right)^2 + \left(2 + \frac{5}{9}\right)^2} \\
   = \sqrt{\left(\frac{23}{18}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{23}{9}\right)^2} \\
   = \sqrt{\frac{529}{324} + \frac{529}{81}} \\
   = \sqrt{\frac{529 + 2116}{324}} \\
   = \sqrt{\frac{2645}{324}} \\
   = \frac{\sqrt{2645}}{18} = \frac{23\sqrt{5}}{18}
   \]

**Answer:** The distance is \( \boxed{\dfrac{23\sqrt{5}}{18}} \).

y =1 + 3=4

The distance from the point P(4,1) to the line 4x - y = 0 along a line making 135 degrees with the x-axis is calculated by finding where this angled line intersects the given line. 

1. **Parametric Equations**: Starting at (4,1), moving in direction (-1,1):
   x = 4 - t  
   y = 1 + t  

2. **Intersection Point**: Substitute into 4x - y =0:  
   4(4 - t) - (1 + t) = 0 → t=3  
   Intersection at (1,4).  

3. **Distance Calculation**: Distance between (4,1) and (1,4):  
   √[(4-1)^2 + (1-4)^2] = √(9+9)=√18=3√2.

The final answer is the distance 3√2 units.

To find the line midway between the given parallel lines \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\) and \(9x + 6y + 18 = 0\), we use the fact that the midline between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\) is given by:

\[ Ax + By + \frac{C_1 + C_2}{2} = 0 \]

Here, the constants are \(C_1 = -7\) and \(C_2 = 18\). Calculating the average of these constants:

\[
\frac{-7 + 18}{2} = \frac{11}{2} = 5.5
\]

Thus, the midline equation is:

\[ 9x + 6y + 5.5 = 0 \]

To eliminate the decimal and present it in a form with integer coefficients, we multiply the entire equation by 2:

\[ 18x + 12y + 11 = 0 \]

Therefore, the line midway between the given lines is:

\[
\boxed{18x + 12y + 11 = 0}
\]

To find the **slope** of the line passing through the points \((6, -4)\) and \((-2, 8)\), we'll use the **slope formula**:

\[
\text{Slope} (m) = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Given Points:**
- Point 1: \((x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)\)
- Point 2: \((x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)\)

\[
m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}
\]

To find the **slope** of the line passing through the points \( (5, 0) \) and \( (9, 0) \), we can use the slope formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   - First point (\( x_1, y_1 \)) = \( (5, 0) \)
   - Second point (\( x_2, y_2 \)) = \( (9, 0) \)

2. **Apply the slope formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{0 - 0}{9 - 5}
   \]

3. **Calculate the difference in y-coordinates and x-coordinates:**
   - \( y_2 - y_1 = 0 - 0 = 0 \)
   - \( x_2 - x_1 = 9 - 5 = 4 \)

4. **Compute the slope:**
   \[
   m = \frac{0}{4} = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The slope of the line is \(\boxed{0}\).

To find the **slope** of the line that makes an inclination of \(120^\circ\) with the positive direction of the x-axis, we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Relationship Between Inclination and Slope
The slope (\(m\)) of a line is given by the tangent of its angle of inclination (\(\theta\)):
\[ m = \tan(\theta) \]

### Step 2: Calculate the Tangent of \(120^\circ\)
Since \(120^\circ\) lies in the second quadrant where tangent values are negative, we can express it as:
\[ \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) \]

We know that:
\[ \tan(60^\circ) = \sqrt{3} \]

Therefore:
\[ \tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3} \]

### Step 3: Determine the Slope
Substituting back into the slope formula:
\[ m = -\sqrt{3} \]

### Final Answer
The slope of the line is:
\[ \boxed{-\sqrt{3}} \]

To find the slope of the other line when the angle between two lines is \( \frac{\pi}{4} \) and one of the lines has a slope of \( \frac{1}{4} \), we can use the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

**Given:**
- \( \theta = \frac{\pi}{4} \), so \( \tan\left( \frac{\pi}{4} \right) = 1 \)
- \( m_1 = \frac{1}{4} \)

**Let the slope of the other line be \( m_2 \).**

Substitute the known values into the formula:

\[
1 = \left| \frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} \right|
\]

This absolute value equation leads to two possible cases:

**Case 1:**
\[
\frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = 1
\]
Solving for \( m_2 \):
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = 1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 = 1 + \frac{1}{4} \\
\frac{3}{4} m_2 = \frac{5}{4} \\
m_2 = \frac{5}{3}
\]

**Case 2:**
\[
\frac{m_2 - \frac{1}{4}}{1 + \frac{1}{4} m_2} = -1
\]
Solving for \( m_2 \):
\[
m_2 - \frac{1}{4} = -1 - \frac{1}{4} m_2 \\
m_2 + \frac{1}{4} m_2 = -1 + \frac{1}{4} \\
\frac{5}{4} m_2 = -\frac{3}{4} \\
m_2 = -\frac{3}{5}
\]

**Final Answer:**
The slope of the other line can be either \( \frac{5}{3} \) or \( -\frac{3}{5} \).

To solve the problem, we'll find the slopes of the two lines and use the property that perpendicular lines have slopes whose product is \(-1\).

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the First Line
The first line passes through the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\). The slope (\(m_1\)) can be calculated using the formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points:

\[
m_1 = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Second Line
Since the two lines are perpendicular, the slope (\(m_2\)) of the second line must satisfy:

\[
m_1 \times m_2 = -1
\]

Substituting \(m_1 = \frac{1}{3}\):

\[
\frac{1}{3} \times m_2 = -1 \\
m_2 = -3
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the Slope of the Line Through (10, 14) and (x, 26)
The second line passes through the points \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\). Using the slope formula:

\[
m_2 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} \\
-3 = \frac{12}{x - 10}
\]

### Step 4: Solve for \(x\)
Multiply both sides by \((x - 10)\):

\[
-3(x - 10) = 12 \\
-3x + 30 = 12 \\
-3x = 12 - 30 \\
-3x = -18 \\
x = 6
\]

### Final Answer
The value of \(x\) is \(\boxed{6}\).

To find the **slope** of a line that makes an angle of \(60^\circ\) with the positive direction of the y-axis measured anticlockwise, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Angle with the x-Axis
The angle made with the y-axis is given as \(60^\circ\). Since the total angle around a point is \(360^\circ\), and considering standard position angles, we can find the corresponding angle \(\theta\) made with the positive direction of the **x-axis**.

Since moving anticlockwise from the positive y-axis:
- The positive x-axis forms an angle of \(90^\circ\) with the positive y-axis.
  
Therefore, if a line makes \(60^\circ\) with the positive y-axis:
\[
\theta = 180^\circ - 60^\circ = 120^\circ
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Slope
The slope \(m\) of a line is given by the tangent of the angle it makes with the positive direction of the x-axis:
\[
m = \tan(\theta)
\]

Substituting \(\theta = 120^\circ\):
\[
m = \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}
\]

### Final Answer
The slope of the line is:
\[
\boxed{-\sqrt{3}}
\]

**Step 1: Find the Slope of the Line**

First, we need to determine the slope (**m**) of the line that passes through the two given points. The formula for calculating the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in our values:
\[
m = \frac{0 - 1}{6 - 5} = \frac{-1}{1} = -1
\]

So, the slope of the line is **-1**.

**Step 2: Calculate the Angle Using Arctangent**

The slope (**m**) of a line is equal to the tangent of the angle (\(\theta\)) it makes with the positive direction of the x-axis. Mathematically, this relationship can be expressed as:

\[
\tan(\theta) = m
\]

We need to find \(\theta\) itself, so we'll use the arctangent function (inverse tangent):

\[
\theta = \arctan(m)
\]

Substituting the value of \(m\):
\[
\theta = \arctan(-1)
\]

**Step 3: Determine the Angle in Degrees**

Using a calculator or trigonometric tables, we know that:

\[
\arctan(1) = 45^\circ
\]

However, since our slope is **-1**, which is negative, this indicates that the angle lies in the second quadrant (between \(90^\circ\) and \(180^\circ\)).

To convert radians to degrees:
\[
\theta = -45^\circ \times \frac{180^\circ}{\pi} = 315^\circ
\]

But typically, angles are considered between \(0^\circ\) and \(180^\circ\), so we can also express this angle as:

\[
\theta = 180^\circ - 45^\circ = 135^\circ
\]

To determine the possible slopes for two lines intersecting at a 45-degree angle (tan(theta)=1), we use the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:

\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]

Since tan(45°) = 1, this equation becomes:

\[
\left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right| = 1
\]

This leads to two possible scenarios for the slopes \(m_1\) and \(m_2\):

1. \( \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} = 1 \)  
   Simplifying: \( m_2 - m_1 = 1 + m_1 m_2 \)  
   Rearranging gives: \( m_2 = \frac{m_1 + 1}{1 - m_1} \)

2. \( \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} = -1 \)  
   Simplifying: \( m_2 - m_1 = -1 - m_1 m_2 \)  
   Rearranging gives: \( m_2 = \frac{m_1 - 1}{1 + m_1} \)

Thus, any two lines with slopes satisfying either of these equations will intersect at a 45-degree angle. 

**Examples:**

- If one line has a slope of 0 (horizontal), the other must have a slope of ±1.
  
- For \(m_1 = 2\), possible values for \(m_2\) are \(-3\) or \(1/3\).

These relationships show that there are infinitely many pairs of lines with slopes satisfying these conditions, each forming a 45-degree angle between them.

**Answer:** The slopes must satisfy either \( m_2 = \frac{m_1 + 1}{1 - m_1} \) or \( m_2 = \frac{m_1 - 1}{1 + m_1} \). Therefore, one possible pair of lines is any two with slopes related by these equations.

\boxed{m_2 = \dfrac{m_1 + 1}{1 - m_1} \text{ or } m_2 = \dfrac{m_1 - 1}{1 + m_1}}

To find the equation of the line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (m):**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form:**
   Using point (3, 1):
   \[
   y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form:**
   \[
   y - 1 = 3x - 9 \\
   y = 3x - 8
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the line is \( y = 3x - 8 \).

Step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify the given points**: (P₁, Q₁) = (114, 1080), (P₂, Q₂) = (116, 1320).

2. **Calculate the slope (m)**:
   - ΔQ = 1320 - 1080 = 240
   - ΔP = 116 - 114 = 2
   - m = ΔQ / ΔP = 240 / 2 = 120.

3. **Formulate the demand equation** using point-slope form:
   - Q - 1080 = 120(P - 114)
   - Simplifying: Q = 120P - (120*114) + 1080
   - Compute constants: 120*114 = 13,680; so Q = 120P - 13,680 + 1,080 → Q = 120P - 12,600.

4. **Find demand at P=117**:
   - Substitute P=117 into the equation: Q = (120*117) - 12,600
   - Calculate 120*117 = 14,040; so Q = 14,040 - 12,600 = 1,440.

**Answer:** The predicted demand at a price of Rs. 117 is **\boxed{1440} litres**.

\( m = -\frac{2}{13} \).

\( y - 13 = -\frac{2}{13}(x - 2) \).

Simplify and rearrange to standard form:
\( 13(y - 13) = -2(x - 2) \)
\( 13y - 169 = -2x + 4 \)
\( 2x + 13y = 173 \).

To find the equation of a line through the point \((0, -3)\) making an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians with the positive x-axis and another parallel line crossing the y-axis 2 units below the origin:

1. **Calculate the slope (m):**
   - The angle is \( \theta = \frac{2\pi}{3} \).
   - Slope, \( m = \tan(\theta) = \tan\left(120^\circ\right) = -\sqrt{3} \).

2. **Find the equation of the first line:**
   - Using point-slope form: \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \).
   - Substituting \( (0, -3) \): \( y + 3 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0) \).
   - Simplifying: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 3 \).

3. **Find the equation of the parallel line crossing the y-axis at (0, -2):**
   - Since it's parallel, slope remains \( m = -\sqrt{3} \).
   - Using point-slope form: \( y + 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0) \).
   - Simplifying: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \).

**Final Answer:**  
The equations of the lines are:
- First line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 3 \)
- Parallel line: \( y = -\sqrt{3}x - 2 \)

</think>

To find the equation of a line passing through the point \((-5, 7)\) and **perpendicular** to the line that passes through the points \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line

First, calculate the slope (\(m\)) of the line passing through \((4, 7)\) and \((-5, 8)\).

The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Plugging in the given points:
\[
m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line

The slope of a line **perpendicular** to another is the negative reciprocal of the original slope.

Given \(m = -\frac{1}{9}\), the perpendicular slope (\(m_{\text{perp}}\)) is:
\[
m_{\text{perp}} = \frac{1}{-(-\frac{1}{9})} = 9
\]

### Step 3: Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Line

The point-slope form of a line's equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m_{\text{perp}}(x - x_1)
\]
where \((x_1, y_1)\) is a point on the line.

Using the point \((-5, 7)\):
\[
y - 7 = 9(x + 5)
\]

### Step 4: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form

Simplify the equation to get it into slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
\[
y - 7 = 9x + 45
\]
\[
y = 9x + 52
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the desired line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 9x + 52}
\]

Next, I need to determine the equation of the median from point R(4, 5) to the midpoint M(0, 2). To find this equation, I'll first calculate the slope of the line passing through points R and M.

m = (5 - 2)/(4 - 0) = 3/4

To find the equation of the median through vertex \( R \) in triangle \( PQR \) with vertices \( P(2, 1) \), \( Q(-2, 3) \), and \( R(4, 5) \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint of Side \( PQ \)

The median from vertex \( R \) will pass through the midpoint of side \( PQ \). Use the midpoint formula:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substituting the coordinates of points \( P \) and \( Q \):
\[
M = \left( \frac{2 + (-2)}{2}, \frac{1 + 3}{2} \right) = \left( \frac{0}{2}, \frac{4}{2} \right) = (0, 2)
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Median \( RM \)

The median connects point \( R(4, 5) \) to midpoint \( M(0, 2) \). Calculate the slope (\( m \)) using the formula:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the coordinates of \( R \) and \( M \):
\[
m = \frac{5 - 2}{4 - 0} = \frac{3}{4}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Median Using Point-Slope Form

The point-slope form of a line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using point \( R(4, 5) \):
\[
y - 5 = \frac{3}{4}(x - 4)
\]

### Step 4: Simplify the Equation

Multiply both sides by 4 to eliminate the fraction:
\[
4(y - 5) = 3(x - 4)
\]
Expanding and simplifying:
\[
4y - 20 = 3x - 12
\]
Bring all terms to one side to form a standard linear equation:
\[
-3x + 4y - 8 = 0
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the median through vertex \( R \) is:

\[
\boxed{-3x + 4y - 8 = 0}
\]

To find the equation of the line that intersects the x-axis at a distance of 5 units to the left of the origin with a slope of \(-4\), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the x-intercept:**
   
   The distance from the origin is 5 units to the left, which corresponds to the point:
   \[
   (-5, 0)
   \]

2. **Use the slope and the x-intercept to find the equation of the line.**

   The general form of a linear equation with slope \(m\) passing through a point \((x_1, y_1)\) is given by:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]

   Substituting \(m = -4\), and the point \((-5, 0)\):
   \[
   y - 0 = -4(x - (-5)) \\
   y = -4(x + 5) \\
   y = -4x - 20
   \]

3. **Final Equation:**
   
   The equation of the line is:
   \[
   \boxed{y = -4x - 20}
   \]

The distance is \boxed{\sqrt{5} - 2}.

To find the distance from the point (1, -3) to the line given by \(2x - \sqrt{5}y = 8\), we use the distance formula for a point to a line. The formula is:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|Ax + By - C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

where the line is given by \(Ax + By = C\).

1. Identify the coefficients \(A\), \(B\), and \(C\) from the line equation:
   - The line equation is \(2x - \sqrt{5}y = 8\), so \(A = 2\), \(B = -\sqrt{5}\), and \(C = 8\).

2. Substitute the coordinates of the point (1, -3) into the formula:
   - Calculate the numerator: \(|2(1) + (-\sqrt{5})(-3) - 8|\)
   - Simplify step by step:
     \[
     |2 + 3\sqrt{5} - 8| = |-6 + 3\sqrt{5}| = |3\sqrt{5} - 6|
     \]
   - Since \(3\sqrt{5} \approx 6.708 > 6\), the absolute value is positive: \(3\sqrt{5} - 6\).

3. Calculate the denominator:
   - Compute \(\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}\):
     \[
     \sqrt{2^2 + (-\sqrt{5})^2} = \sqrt{4 + 5} = \sqrt{9} = 3
     \]

4. Divide the numerator by the denominator:
   - The distance is:
     \[
     \frac{|3\sqrt{5} - 6|}{3} = \frac{3(\sqrt{5} - 2)}{3} = \sqrt{5} - 2
     \]

Thus, the exact distance from the point (1, -3) to the line is \(\boxed{\sqrt{5} - 2}\).

To find the equation of the **right bisector** (perpendicular bisector) of the line segment joining the points \((5, 6)\) and \((1, 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Midpoint
The midpoint \(M\) of a segment with endpoints \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
M = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Plugging in the points:
\[
M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
\]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Original Line Segment
The slope \(m\) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Calculate it for the given points:
\[
m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = 0.5
\]

### Step 3: Find the Slope of the Perpendicular Bisector
The slope of a line perpendicular to another with slope \(m\) is:
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m}
\]
So,
\[
m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{0.5} = -2
\]

### Step 4: Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Perpendicular Bisector
The point-slope form of a line is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using the midpoint \((3, 5)\) and the slope \(-2\):
\[
y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
\]

### Step 5: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 5 = -2x + 6 \\
y = -2x + 11
\]
Thus, the equation of the perpendicular bisector is:
\[
\boxed{y = -2x + 11}
\]

First, I need to determine the slope of the given line \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \). Rewriting it in slope-intercept form gives me \( y = (1/7)x + 5/7 \), so the slope is \( 1/7 \).

Since the desired line is perpendicular, its slope will be the negative reciprocal of \( 1/7 \), which is \( -7 \).

Next, using the point-slope form with a slope of \( -7 \) and an x-intercept at \( (5, 0) \), I can write the equation as:
\( y - 0 = -7(x - 5) \).

**Solution:**

We are tasked with finding the equation of a line that is perpendicular to the line given by:
\[ x - 7y + 5 = 0 \]
and has an **x-intercept** at \( (5, 0) \).

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line

First, we'll rewrite the given equation in slope-intercept form (\( y = mx + b \)) to identify its slope.

Starting with:
\[ x - 7y + 5 = 0 \]
Subtract \( x \) and 5 from both sides:
\[ -7y = -x - 5 \]
Divide every term by \(-7\):
\[ y = \frac{1}{7}x + \frac{5}{7} \]

From this, we can see that the **slope (\( m \))** of the given line is:
\[ m = \frac{1}{7} \]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line

The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the **negative reciprocal** of the original slope. Therefore:

\[ \text{Slope of perpendicular line} = -\frac{1}{m} = -\frac{1}{\frac{1}{7}} = -7 \]

### Step 3: Use the Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation

We know that the desired line has a slope (\( m' \)) of \( -7 \) and passes through the point \( (5, 0) \).

The **point-slope form** of a line is:
\[ y - y_1 = m'(x - x_1) \]

Substituting the known values:
\[ y - 0 = -7(x - 5) \]
Simplify:
\[ y = -7x + 35 \]

### Step 4: Convert to Standard Form

To express this in standard form (\( Ax + By + C = 0 \)):

Starting from the slope-intercept form:
\[ y = -7x + 35 \]
Add \( 7x \) to both sides:
\[ 7x + y = 35 \]
Subtract 35 from both sides to set the equation to zero:
\[ 7x + y - 35 = 0 \]

### Final Answer

The equation of the line perpendicular to \( x - 7y + 5 = 0 \) with an **x-intercept** at \( (5, 0) \) is:

\[
\boxed{7x + y - 35 = 0}
\]

   \[
   5(-4) - 6(5) + C = 0 \\
   -20 - 30 + C = 0 \\
   C = 50
   \]
3. Thus, the equation of the parallel line is:
   \[
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
