The distance between two parallel lines given by equations \( ax + by = c_1 \) and \( ax + by = c_2 \) is calculated using the formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|c_1 - c_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Applying this to the given lines:
- Line 1: \( 19x + 12y = 38 \)
- Line 2: \( 19x + 12y = -35 \)

We find:

\[
|c_1 - c_2| = |38 - (-35)| = 73
\]
\[
\sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{19^2 + 12^2} = \sqrt{361 + 144} = \sqrt{505}
\]

Thus, the distance is:

\[
\frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \text{ or } \frac{73\sqrt{505}}{505}
\]

To express the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\) in intercept form:

1. Divide both sides by 18:
   \[
   \frac{12x}{18} - \frac{9y}{18} = 1
   \]
   
2. Simplify the fractions:
   \[
   \frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
   \]

3. Rewrite in intercept form by separating terms:
   \[
   \frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
   \]
   
Thus, the equation is in intercept form with \(a = \frac{3}{2}\) and \(b = -2\). The x-intercept is at \(\left(\frac{3}{2}, 0\right)\), and the y-intercept is at \((0, -2)\).

The final answer is:
- Intercept form: \(\boxed{\dfrac{x}{1.5} + \dfrac{y}{-2} = 1}\)  
- x-intercept: \(\boxed{\left( \dfrac{3}{2}, 0 \right)}\)  
- y-intercept: \(\boxed{(0, -2)}\)

To solve the problem of finding the **distance along the line** \(2x – y = 0\) from the point \((1, 2)\) to another point on this line, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify Two Points on the Line
The given line is:
\[
2x - y = 0
\]
Let's choose a value for \(x\) and solve for \(y\) to find a point on the line.

- Let \(x = 1\):
\[
2(1) - y = 0 \implies y = 2
\]
So, one point is:
\[
(1, 2)
\]

### Step 2: Determine Another Point on the Line
Since both points must lie on the line \(2x – y = 0\), let's choose another value for \(x\) to find a second point.

- Let \(x = 3\):
\[
2(3) - y = 0 \implies y = 6
\]
So, another point is:
\[
(3, 6)
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the Distance Between the Two Points
Now, use the distance formula between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\):
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Plugging in the coordinates:
\[
\begin{align*}
\text{Distance} &= \sqrt{(3 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2} \\
&= \sqrt{2^2 + 4^2} \\
&= \sqrt{4 + 16} \\
&= \sqrt{20} \\
&= 2\sqrt{5}
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer
The distance along the line \(2x – y = 0\) from the point \((1, 2)\) to another point on the same line is:

\[
\boxed{2\,\sqrt{5}}
\]

To find the distance from point P(4, 1) to the line 4x - y = 0 measured along a line that makes an angle of 135° with the positive x-axis, we follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Equation of the Measuring Line:**
   - The given angle is 135°, so the slope (m) is tan(135°) = -1.
   - Using point-slope form with P(4, 1), the equation is y - 1 = -1(x - 4), which simplifies to y = -x + 5.

2. **Find Intersection Point:**
   - Solve the system of equations:
     - Original line: 4x - y = 0 => y = 4x
     - Measuring line: y = -x + 5
   - Setting 4x = -x + 5, we get x = 1 and y = 4. So the intersection point is (1, 4).

3. **Calculate Distance Between P(4, 1) and (1, 4):**
   - Using the distance formula: √[(4-1)² + (1-4)²] = √[9 + 9] = √18 = 3√2.

**Final Answer**
The midline of S has equation \boxed{18x + 12y + 11 = 0}.
</think>

To find the midline of set \( S \), we start with the given lines:

1. The first line is \( 3x + 2y = \frac{7}{3} \).
2. The second line is \( 3x + 2y = -6 \).

We need to find a line that is equidistant from both of these lines. 

First, we rewrite the equations in standard form:
- The first line can be rewritten as \( 9x + 6y = 7 \) by multiplying both sides by 3.
- The second line remains \( 3x + 2y = -6 \).

Next, we find a line that is equidistant from these two lines. We use the concept of the midline between two parallel lines. For any point on this midline, the distance to each original line should be equal.

We calculate the midline by averaging the constants of the given equations after scaling them to have the same coefficients:
- The first equation scaled is \( 9x + 6y = 7 \).
- The second equation scaled is \( 3x + 2y = -6 \).

To find the midline, we average the constants:
- Convert both lines to a common form: 
  - First line: \( 9x + 6y = 7 \)
  - Second line: \( 9x + 6y = -18 \) (after multiplying by 3)

The midline will be halfway between these two constants:
- The average of 7 and -18 is \(\frac{7 + (-18)}{2} = \frac{-11}{2}\).

Thus, the equation of the midline in standard form is \( 9x + 6y = -\frac{11}{2} \). To clear the fraction, we multiply through by 2:

\[ 18x + 12y + 11 = 0 \]

Therefore, the equation of the midline is:
\[
\boxed{18x + 12y + 11 = 0}
\]
